>> Okay. Thank you very much. Uh I want to

talk today about applications of active

inference in biology and particularly

the issue of modeling the environment as

an active inference agent which the FEP

in fact requires us to do.

Um but the approach I want to take is

via the symmetries of the FEP

and I want to focus in particular on two

symmetries of the FEP and hence the

active inference formalism.

One is that all components of any

decomposition of any system are active

inference agents and need to be treated

as such.

And the other symmetry is that all

boundaries between systems, so all

Markoff blankets for example in the

classical picture are information

symmetric.

Both of these symmetries are universal.

Uh they come just from the physics, but

they're both especially relevant to

thinking of active inference in a

biological or social setting.

So in in thinking about

any interactions between living systems

and their environments

um these two symmetries have to be taken

fairly seriously from a modeling

methodology point of view.

So where we're going today is first to

system, some kind of universe

and seeing where that physics takes us.

And this is kind of an unfamiliar

And then the other example will be

your problem and giving it to your

And then finally u I'll talk about some

of um this way of thinking and in

So, um,

top down physics, we're just going to

assume three things.

We're going to assume that any, um,

system that we're interested in can be

described with a finite Hilbert space.

So, a Hilbert space is just a space

whose basis vectors are the possible

values of the possible degrees of

freedom of the system.

So if you think of asking a system a

bunch of yes no questions

every yes no question corresponds to one

of the basis vectors of this state space

if you use a hilbert spaces the state

space

and therefore we can think of any of

these spaces as just spaces of cubits

quantum bits u because those are the

most fundamental entity to which you can

ask a yes no question and get an answer

either yes or no.

So we'll assume that all systems can be

described in this way and we'll assume

that they're all finite dimensional and

that's just for convenience. They can be

arbitrarily large but if we assume

they're finite dimensional we don't have

to worry about singularities which just

cause problems.

The second assumption we'll make is that

there's some sort of background time

parameter. There's some

um

intuitive concept of time that we can

use to talk about change. Uh

and we will not assume that this is

measurable by any experiment. So it's

it's just a parameter that we'll use in

the theory and measurable times have to

be constructed but they're constructed

from this back background time parameter

and the way to think about that is a

measurable time requires a clock and for

a clock to be meaningful it has to

change and to talk about change we have

to assume some sort of background time.

So all clocks run slower than the

fundamental frequency of the background

time parameter that we're assuming.

Then the third assumption we're going to

make is that isolated systems conserve

information.

And this is the principle of unitarity

which is the fundamental principle of

quantum theory.

Um it says that if you have an isolated

system and its state is evolving in this

background time

then the state will encode exactly the

same amount of information at every

time.

Um

that

assumption

uh if you throw out that assumption then

Uh if you abandon unitarity

um

up to the three assumptions that we've

So this symmetry in fact is key to the

free energy principle. And now we can

state the free energy principle.

It says that if we slice this system U,

if we slice any isolated system anywhere

to define two components S and E,

then if S and E are to remain in a

separable joint state, so if they're

going to remain mutually statistically

independent,

quant Quantum theory we call

lack of entanglement.

So if S and E are going to be separate

systems, things that we can talk about

independently, they're not entangled. So

they have to behave in a way that is

consistent with them not being

entangled.

and maintaining separability

not becoming entangled requires keeping

the interaction weak with respect to the

internal interactions.

So we can express that by thinking of

the dimensions of the interaction which

are the same as the dimensions of the

Hilbert spaces.

the dimension of the interaction is the

same as the dimension of this boundary

that I've used to slice the system.

And that boundary just has to be much

smaller than either of the systems that

it separates.

Um, and that is intuitively obvious. The

boundary has to be small enough that the

systems robustly have internal states or

the FE doesn't tell you anything. The FE

requires that systems have internal

states.

But this gives us a an an important

qualitative fact which is that

sensations and actions which are defined

at the boundary are lowdimensional

projections of the general models of the

systems because the general models are

implemented by these internal

interactions.

So when we think about what a system is

observing or what a system is doing

in both cases those are very

lowdimensional representations of its of

its generative model lowdimensional

inputs lowdimensional outputs.

So with this we can now think about the

symmetries of the FEP.

So the first symmetry is that

if E is the environment of S, then S is

the environment of E.

So if S's generative model, its internal

dynamics, is predicting E's action on

the boundary,

then E's generative model, which is its

internal dynamics, is predicting S's

actions on the boundary.

And E's actions on the boundary are S's

perceptions or sensations.

U S's actions on the boundary are E's

perceptions or sensations.

So the boundary is the locust of all of

the sensations or actions of SNE.

The boundary is the markoff blanket

between them which encodes sensation and

action in the standard formulation of

the FE.

So both of these systems are constantly

acting to minimize variational free

energy defined at the same boundary.

So this minimization of VF

is always a co-minimization

being undertaken by both the system and

its environment.

uh and the reason is that both a system

and its environment are stri trying to

stay separate from each other. They're

trying to keep the environment well-

definfined. They're trying to keep the

boundary well- definfined. And the only

way to do that is to keep this

interaction weak. It's to keep the the

interaction from kind of blowing up in

their faces and tearing the boundary

apart.

So that's the first symmetry of the FEP.

If the system's an agent, the

environment's an agent, too.

The second symmetry is that this

interaction being well defined at the

boundary requires that the information

exchange be symmetric.

So, uh again, this interaction is an

operator

um that acts at the boundary to move

information between

S and E.

And it's the same operator

from whichever direction you look at it.

And the operator being well- definfined

is the same condition as the boundary

being well defined.

And in fact, we can see that intuitively

by thinking of the boundary as a cubit

space. Again, it's a finite dimensional

Hbert space. And if we think of the

some set of cubits.

perfectly symmetrical

uh sets of operators.

Um sort of the the S half of HSC and the

and each is doing preparation and

measurement of each of those cubits.

So the boundary being well defined, the

this symmetry and you can think of that

just as Newton's third law u of equal

and opposite response to any imposed

force.

So this again is true of everything.

Hi, I'm David and I'm going to talk

briefly about bounded rationality and

multi- aent alignment.

So to begin with some motivation,

a lot of focus in alignment research is

centered on this question of how do we

align a single potentially superhuman

level AI with humans or humanity.

The big issue here aside from how we

define these things is that there's no

agreed upon solution to this problem and

it seemed like a very difficult one in

general.

On the other hand, a lot of focus in

capabilities research centered on the

question of how we build a single

superhuman level AI system.

The reality is that for such systems

there are significant trade-offs between

performance and cost.

Moreover, AI agents are increasingly

being deployed on the internet to carry

out tasks autonomously over longer and

longer horizons.

Fundamentally, we existed and are

building towards a multi-polar world

which is an ecosystem of many AIs,

humans and organizations all interacting

with each other.

And so a central question here is how do

we ensure that this ecosystem is

aligned, beneficial, efficient and

resilient.

The hypothesis I want to explore here is

that we start from the fact that a major

source of risk from advanced AI comes

from this extreme uninterruptible

optimization towards specific goals.

Now if a significant proportion of the

AI agents in this ecosystem

are highly capable, have access to

significant amounts of resources and are

insufficiently aligned. This could

create destabilizing undesirable

outcomes.

And so perhaps complimentary to working

on alignment, maybe we should also think

about limiting capabilities and

resources within such ecosystems

until and as we progress with alignment.

So what this what could this lead to?

Well, one of our essential hypotheses is

that this could lead to an ecosystem of

specialized, efficient, and reliable

agents all working together alongside

humans to solve problems

much in the same way as we would see

organizations

or societies do.

So, how might we reason about such

systems? Well, one concept that we

propose that's potentially useful is out

of bounded rationality.

So what is bound rationality?

Well, the fundamental idea here is that

real world decision-m is subject to

limitations.

These limitations can come in the form

of limited time, energy, money,

computational resources, attention,

memory, information, etc.

And crucially, decision-m need to take

these factors into account if it is to

be effective in the real world.

Now, boundary rationality was originally

intended as a descriptive model of human

decision-m then was later expanded to

encompass both biological and artificial

decision makers as well as to bridge the

gap between both normative and

descriptive theories of decision-m.

The key mechanism here is that of

satisficing

which is related to finding the optimal

solution in a simplified model or

finding a good enough solution in a more

realistic model.

And we see these kinds of trade-offs

manifest

in the modern day in the form of cost

performance trade-offs especially with

the rise of test time inference. So here

we see several graphs from major AI

companies, researchers and also public

benchmarks that are increasingly focused

on the trade-off between performance on

a certain task or benchmark and the

costs in terms of test time or training

time compute or in terms of dollars for

inference

etc.

So

just for a brief timeline of bounded

rationality,

the concept

really got started with Herbert Simon

who introduced it in the 1950s and wrote

a lot of seinal works on this.

Then sort of the 1970s and 80s, it was

later extended and really fleshed out by

Amos Terski and Daniel Canaman in their

research program on heristics and

cognitive biases. as well as their

distinction between system one and

system two thinking.

Then later in the 90s you have good

gigarenzer who used concepts of an

adaptive toolbox and ecological

rationality

as well as to Russell

who worked on bounded optimality

and you know since then especially in

the 2000s up to now there's a

proliferation of approaches and ways of

modeling this

some of which I'll talk about next.

So one of the key concepts here is

computational rationality

and the basic idea is that we want to

select the optimal program for some

valid machine to execute.

And so you can imagine in this diagram

on the left here, you have some sort of

utility function in an environment from

which you select an optimal program

within a space of feasible programs

and you can study the optimal behavior

for such programs and connected to

observed organism behaviors.

On the right here you see another way of

thinking about this idea of

computational rationality where you have

your utility which is diminishing with a

cost of delays.

And this trade-off means that there's an

optimal

time for you to run your computation

before you select an action.

And a key concept here is that of meta

level reasoning

which focuses on focuses on considering

the current uncertainties the time

critical losses with continuing in

computation and the expected gains in

precision of reasoning with additional

computation.

Now another concept that's been

increasingly

popular in recent years is that of

resource rationality.

And the key idea here is that we were

trying to find the optimal way for an

agent to use its limited resources.

So you can imagine this triangle here

representing a space of possible lines

that are implementable with our brain's

limited resources and on the y- axis you

have performance of these different

lines. The resource rational approach is

to find the optimal one within this

feasible set.

And some of the key steps associated

with resource rational analysis is that

first you define the problem at the

computational level. Then you postulate

a class of algorithms that the mind

might use to approximately solve the

problem.

The cost of computational resources and

the utility of accuracy of the

algorithms.

After that you want to find the

algorithm in the class that best trades

off resources and accuracy.

Then you evaluate predictions of this

algorithm against empirical data. And if

there's a gap, you go back to step one

or two.

Now, another concept within this space

is that of information theoretic under

rationality.

And the key idea here is that we account

for the cost of cognition using tools

and concepts of information theory.

In particular,

agents start with some prior probability

distribution Q over choices and does

some computation or information

processing to transform it into a

posterior P.

And you can measure the cost of this

transformation as the K divergence from

the prior to posterior.

And so agent optimizes an objective

which is an expected utility minus a

regularizer which represents the

information.

So few questions related to boundary

rationality in alignment

in this context of ecosystems is first

of all how do we build these specialized

efficient and reliable agents.

Secondly, how do we coordinate such

agents with each other and with humans

or organizations to reliably and safely

solve more complex problems?

Finally, how do we mitigate the risk

that might emerge critically in the

context of these multiation systems?

Now, our view is that this is an

important research agenda

and we hope that we are able to dedicate

resources and talent towards solving

these important problems.
